Subtraction rule
When a smaller-value symbol appears
immediately before a large-value symbol,
smaller-value is subtracted from the larger-
value.

wvq lrkq ,efí'

XL = 50 – 10 = 40
XC = 100 – 10 = 90

  (−255₁₀ to +255₁₀)
• 10 bits – 1000000000 to 0111111111
  (−511₁₀ to +511₁₀)

Relationship Between Addition and
Subtraction
tl;= lsÍu iy wvq lsÍu w;r iïnkaO;djh

Addition and subtraction are inverse arithmetic
operations. In many cases, the same result can
be obtained by replacing one operation with
the other when the sign of the second number
is changed.
tl;= lsÍu iy wvq lsÍu hkq m%;sf,dau wxl
.‚;uh fufyhqï fõ' fndfyda wjia:dj,§"
fojk ixLHdfõ ,l=K fjkia lr tla fufyhqula
fjkqjg fjk;a fufyhqula fhdod .ekSfuka tu
m%;sM,hu ,nd .; yel'

 Example
           5 − 2 = 5 + (−2) = 3
           5 + 2 = 5 − (−2) = 7
           −5 − 2 = −5 + (−2) = −7
           −5 + 2 = −5 − (−2) = −3

to represent positive and negative integers in              up to 255.

                                                            2 bits      -2 to +1

NOTE:                                                       from 0 to 1 and acts as the sign bit.

Mathematical Derivation of 1's Complement             Therefore, / tu ksid
and 2's Complement Subtraction.
1 ys wkqmQrl iy 2 ys wkqmQrl wkqmQrl l%ufõoj,           X−Y = X+ (1’s complement of Y) + 1− 2n

Why IEEE Does This / IEEE fufia lrkafka wehs             Single Precision / ;ks ksrjoH;djh

easier than comparing signed numbers.                    ;ks ksrjoH;djh i|yd ìgq 32la Ndú; lrhs'

                                                         (K=127).

The exponent field has 8 bits. An 8-bit unsigned         Why It's Called "Precision"
number ranges from 0 to 255.                             fuh ˜ksrjoH;djh˜ f,i y÷kajkafka wehs
o¾Yl lafIa;%hg ìgq 8la we;' ìgq 8l ,l=Kq rys;            Precision refers to the number of significant binary

NOTE 02:
BCD is an encoding system, not a number
system. Decimal and Binary are number
systems. BCD shows how to represent decimal
digits using binary codes.

4. Unicode                                              It solved the problem of incompatible character
                                                        encodings. Before Unicode,
                                                        th tlsfkl yd fkd.e,fmk wlaIr fla;k ms<sn|
                                                        .eg¨j úi£h' hqksfldaâ j,g fmr"

                                                         • Each language/region often had its own
                                                           encoding.
                                                           iEu NdIdjlgu$m%foaYhlgu fndfyda úg
                                                           ;ukaf.au fla;khla ;snq‚'
Unicode was created to establish one universal           • One encoding could not represent text
standard for representing text in computers.               from multiple languages at once.
mß.Kl ;=< fm< ksrEmKh lsÍu i|yd tla                        tla fla;khlg tljr NdId lsysmhl fm<

lrk ,§'
                                                         • Exchanging documents between systems
Before Unicode, different countries, languages,            risked corrupting the text.
and software systems used different character              moaO;s w;r f,aLk yqjudre lr .ekSfï§
encoding standards. Such as,                               fm< úkdY ùfï wjodkula ;snq‚'
hqksfldaâ j,g fmr" úúO rgj,a" NdId iy
uDÿldx. moaO;s úúO wlaIr fla;k iïu;hka                  Unicode fixed this by creating one universal
Ndú;d l<y'                                              standard where each character gets a fixed,
                                                        unique identity regardless of platform,
ASCII           EBCDIC          ISO 8859
                                                        software, or language.
Shift-JIS       Big5                                    fõÈldj" uDÿldx.h fyda NdIdj l=ula jqj;a iEu
                                                        wlaIrhlgu iaÓr" wkkH wkkH;djhla ,efnk
These standards were often incompatible,                tla úYaùh iïu;hla iE§fuka hqksfldaâ fuh
causing text to appear incorrectly when                 ksjerÈ lf<ah'
exchanged between different systems.
fuu iïu;hka fndfyda úg tlsfkl yd                        Code Point / fla; ,laIHh

lsÍfï§ jerÈ f,i o¾Ykh ùug fya;= úh'                     A code point is a unique number Unicode

Bitwise operators                                                          A                            NOT A
ìgq wkqidß fufyhqïldrl                                                     0                                1
                                                                           1                                0
Bitwise operators perform logical operations on
individual bits of binary numbers.
ìgq wkqidß fufyhqïldrl uÕska oaúuh ixLHdj,                     Example :
;ks ìgq u; ;¾ldkql+, fufyhqï isÿ lrhs'
                                                               4-bit Representation
The term “bitwise” means that an operation is                  +510=01012
performed one bit at a time.                                   = NOT (01012)
ìgq wkqidß; hk mofhka woyia jkafka hï
fufyhqula jrlg tla ìgqj ne.ska isÿ lrk njhs'
                                                               N3 = 01012           0           1           0           1
Each bit is processed independently according                    NOT(N3)            1           0           1           0
to the corresponding logical rule.                                             NOT (01012) = 10102
wod< ;¾lk kS;shg wkqj iEu ìgqjlau iajdëkj
ieliSug ,la flfra'                                             Unsigned interpretation: 10102=1010
                                                               Signed (4-bit two's complement)
NOT operates on one binary number, while                       interpretation: 10102=−610
AND, OR and XOR operate on two binary
numbers of equal length.
                                                               8-bit Representation
NOT fufyhqu tla oaúuh ixLHdjla u; l%shd;aul
                                                               +510=000001012

                                                               = NOT (000001012)
È.lska hq;a oaúuh ixLHd folla u; l%shd;aul fõ'

When two binary numbers have different                         N4 = 01012       0       0   0       0   0       1   0       1
numbers of bits, leading zeros are added to the                  NOT(N4)        1       1   1       1   1       0   1       0
shorter number to make both numbers equal in
                                                                      NOT (000001012) = 111110102
length before performing the bitwise operation.

                                                               Unsigned interpretation: 111110102=25010

ixLHdfõ bÈßhg ìxÿ tl;= lrkq ,efí'                              interpretation: 111110102=−610

Bitwise operators perform logical operations on                NOTE:
binary digits (bits), not on decimal numbers.                  The same decimal number may produce
ìgquh fufyhqïldrl uÕska ;¾ldkql+, fufyhqï                      different Bitwise NOT results when represented

Bitwise NOT                                                    ,nd Èh yelsh'

This operation inverts each bit of a binary                    The decimal value of the result also depends on
number. If a bit is 0, it becomes 1, and if it's 1, it         whether it is interpreted as an unsigned or
becomes 0                                                      signed (two's complement) number.

The Bitwise NOT operator processes each bit
independently and does not require a second
operand.

                                                           1 GB     = 1024 MB
Data measuring units are used to describe the
                                                                    = 1024 × 1 MB
amount of data stored, processed, or
                                                                    = 1024 × 1024 KB
transferred in a computer system in a clear and
standard way.                                                       = 1024 × 1024 x 1 KB

                   = 1 Bit                                          = 233 bits
4 bits = ½ Byte = 1 Nibble
                                                           2 TB     = 2 x 1 TB
8 bits             = 1 Byte
                                                                    = 2 x 1024 GB
1024 Bytes         = 1 KiloByte
                                                                    = 2 x 1024 x 1 GB
1024 KB            = 1 MegaByte
                                                                    = 2 x 1024 x 1024 MB
1024 MB            = 1 GigaByte
                                                                    = 2 x 1024 x 1024 x 1 MB
1024 GB            = 1 TeraByte
                                                                    = 2 x 1024 x 1024 x 1024 KB
1024 TB            = 1 PetaByte
                                                                    = 2 x 1024 x 1024 x 1024 x 1 KB
                                                                    = 2 x 1024 x 1024 x 1024 x 1024 Bytes

computer. It can hold only one binary value,                        = 2 x1024 x 1024 x 1024x 1024 x 1 Bytes
either 0 or 1.                                                      = 2 x 1024 x 1024 x 1024 x 1024 x 8 bits
